DIA-Estimator and Multidimensional Model Misspecifications: GNSS-based Positioning Safety Analysis for UAVs
Authors: Sebastian Ciuban, Peter J. G. Teunissen, Christian C. J. M. Tiberius
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
The Detection, Identification, and Adaptation (DIA)-estimator integrates parameter estimation and hypothesis testing for model misspecifications. This contribution presents a positioning safety analysis approach grounded in the DIA-estimator framework, with a particular emphasis on multidimensional model misspecifications, such as simultaneous outliers in the observations. While recent work has focused on the performance of the detection and identification of multidimensional model misspecifications, we turn our attention to how they affect the probability density function (PDF) of the DIA-estimator and, consequently, the probability of positioning failure–an indicator relevant for safety-of-life applications (e.g., automotive, aviation, rail, maritime). This work formulates and quantifies the probability of positioning failure and its conditional components. A representative simulation-based study is presented for a UAV equipped with a GPS receiver configured to achieve performance comparable to Technical Standard Order (TSO)-certified receivers. The analysis is carried out for two scenarios: a fixed GPS satellite geometry at a single time snapshot, and for a varying GPS satellite geometry over a 24-hour period over an authorized UAV airspace region in the Netherlands using real satellite ephemeris data. Together, these scenarios provide insights into the structure of the DIA-estimator’s PDF, such as multimodality and orientation with respect to the chosen positioning safety region, and support comprehensive evaluation of positioning safety. Although the current focus is on GPS-based positioning, the presented approach is general and can be extended to include multisensor configurations, additional GNSS constellations, and applied to other safety-critical applications, which are subjects of future work.

Introduction

The Detection, Identification, and Adaptation (DIA)-estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\underline{\text {x}}} \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}, introduced by Teunissen, is part of a unifying framework that captures the interplay between parameter estimation and hypothesis testing in the context of the DIA method (Teunissen 2018). Parameter estimation is performed to obtain estimates of the parameters of interest, while hypothesis testing is used to validate these results and adapt the underlying models in the case of misspecifications. The DIA method has found applications across a wide range of domains, such as in GNSS-based positioning (Bakker and Tiberius 2017; Zaminpardaz et al. 2019; Ciuban et al. 2024) quality control in geodetic networks (DGCC 1984), deformation monitoring (Zaminpardaz et al. 2020), quality control in navigation system (Teunissen 1990), and, more recently, in positioning safety analyses for automated and autonomous driving (Ciuban et al. 2025a), among others.

Accounting for the occurrence of multidimensional misspecifications (e.g., multiple outliers) when deciding on the alternative models (hypotheses) for the DIA method is of particular interest, as it more accurately reflects real-world conditions. Recent studies have investigated the performance of the testing procedure with respect to its ability to detect and identify multiple outliers (Zaminpardaz and Teunissen 2023; Yu et al. 2023, 2024). However, these studies have primarily focused on the detection and identification aspects, without considering how such multidimensional misspecifications affect the probability density function (PDF) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\overline{\underline{\text {x}}}}(x)$$\end{document} of the DIA-estimator. This consideration is particularly important for positioning safety analyses in safety-of-life applications (e.g., automotive, aviation, rail, maritime), where the probability of positioning failure is of interest (Ciuban et al. 2024, 2025, 2025)1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}} = \textsf{P}\left( \overline{\underline{\mathrm{x}}} \in \mathcal {B}^c \right) =\int _{\mathcal {B}^c} f_{\overline{\underline{\text {x}}}}(x) d \textit{x}, $$\end{document}with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F} = \{\overline{\underline{\text {x}}} \in \mathcal {B}^c\}$$\end{document} being the event of positioning failure (Special Committee 159, R.T.C.A. 2020) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}^c \subset \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document} is the complement of an application specific safety-region \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B} \subset \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}. In this contribution, we present an approach for performing positioning safety analyses aimed at quantifying (1) and its conditional components, while accounting for multidimensional model misspecifications caused by outliers in the observations.

With respect to our previous research (Ciuban et al. 2024, 2025a, 2025b), the main contributions are as follows: (i) analysis of the impact of multidimensional outliers on the shape of the conditional components of the DIA-estimator’s \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\overline{\underline{\text {x}}}}(x)$$\end{document}; (ii) consideration of time-varying positioning functional and stochastic models (e.g., due to the evolution of GPS satellite geometry over 24 h) into the computation of the probability of positioning failure; (iii) application of the DIA estimator’s framework to Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) cases, demonstrating its relevance in this domain.

To illustrate the approach for positioning safety analysis, we present a representative case study for an Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) assumed to be equipped with a GPS receiver configured to achieve positioning performance comparable to that of receivers certified under the Technical Standard Order (TSO) for GPS-based UAV operations (FAA 2017; Cozzens 2021). The analysis is carried out for two scenarios. The first scenario considers a GPS satellite geometry as observed by the UAV’s GPS receiver at a snapshot of time, enabling detailed analysis of the conditional components of the DIA-estimator’s PDF relative to the shape of the safety region \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B} \subset \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}, as well as the computed components of the probability of positioning failure. In the second scenario we consider a 24-hours (on May 24, 2024) evolution of GPS satellites moving over an airspace region in The Netherlands for which authorization can be obtained for UAV operations (EASA online). Together, these scenarios provide insights into the behavior of the DIA-estimator’s PDF and, consequently, into the components of the probability of positioning failure–supporting a comprehensive evaluation of positioning safety. The presented positioning safety analysis is consistent with scenario-based safety assessment frameworks, which are widely used in domains such as automated and autonomous vehicles (Riedmaier 2020; United Nations Economic Commission for Europe 2023; Gelder 2024) and UAVs (Khatiri 2023), among others.

This contribution is organized as follows: In Section 2 we briefly review the main principles of the DIA-estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\underline{\text {x}}} \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document} and of its PDF \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\overline{\underline{\text {x}}}}(x)$$\end{document}. In Section 3 we present the formulation of the probability of positioning failure and its components. Section 4 presents the positioning-safety analysis for a UAV at a single snapshot of time and over a period of 24 h. Section 6 contains the summary and the concluding remarks of this contribution. Throughout the paper we make use of the following notation: an underscore denotes a random quantity (e.g., the random vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {y}} \in \mathbb {R}^m$$\end{document}), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\underline{\text {y}}}(\textit{y})$$\end{document} is the PDF of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {y}}\in \mathbb {R}^m$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{E}_{f_{\underline{\mathrm{y}}}}\left( \underline{\mathrm{y}} \right) = \int _{\mathbb {R}^m} \mathrm{y} f_{\underline{\mathrm{y}}}(\textit{y}) d\textit{y}$$\end{document} is the expected value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {y}}\in \mathbb {R}^m$$\end{document}. The joint PDF of two random vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {x}} \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {y}} \in \mathbb {R}^m$$\end{document} is denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\underline{\text {x}},\underline{\text {y}}}(x,y)$$\end{document}. A projection matrix is denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Pi }_{\text {A}}$$\end{document} and it projects orthogonally onto the range space of the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {A} \in \mathbb {R}^{m \times n}$$\end{document} (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}(\text {A})$$\end{document}). For the weighted squared norm of a vector we use the notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$||\underline{\text {y}}||^2_{\text {Q}_{\text {y} \text {y}}} = \underline{\text {y}}^T \text {Q}^{-1}_{\text {y} \text {y}} \underline{\text {y}}$$\end{document}. If the squared norm is with respect to (w.r.t.) the identity matrix then it is denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$||.||^2$$\end{document}. A normal PDF is denoted as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}(.,.)$$\end{document}, a chi-squared PDF as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi ^2(.,.)$$\end{document}, and a uniform PDF as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}(.,.)$$\end{document}.

Review of DIA-Estimator

This section provides a concise review of the principles of combined parameter estimation and statistical hypothesis testing, as established in the theoretical framework of the DIA-estimator (Teunissen 2018). We start by considering a linear(ized) observation model with normally (Gaussian) distributed observables (Odijk 2017; Maaref et al. 2021; Liu et al. 2023),2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \underline{\text {y}} \in \mathbb {R}^m \sim \mathcal {N}\left( \textsf{E}_{f_{\underline{\text {y}}}}\left( \underline{\text {y}} \right) , \text {Q}_{\text {y} \text {y}} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where misspecifications could occur in the mean \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{E}_{f_{\underline{\text {y}}}}\left( \underline{\text {y}} \right) \in \mathbb {R}^m$$\end{document}, vc-matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Q}_{\text {y} \text {y}} \in \mathbb {R}^{m \times m}$$\end{document}, and/or in the assumed type of the probability distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\underline{\text {y}}}(y)$$\end{document}, meaning the Gaussian assumption may not hold. In this contribution, we focus on the case of misspecifications in the mean, as these are the most common in practice (e.g., caused by outliers) (Teunissen 2017). To account for such misspecifications of the observation model, the following multiple hypothesis testing problem is formulated3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {H}_0: \textsf{E}_{f_{\underline{\text {y}}}}\left( \underline{\text {y}} \right) = \text {A} \text {x}~~~\text {vs.}~~~\mathcal {H}_{i \ne 0}: \textsf{E}_{f_{\underline{\text {y}}}}\left( \underline{\text {y}} \right) = \text {A} \text {x} + \text {C}_i \text {b}_i, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {A} \in \mathbb {R}^{m \times n}$$\end{document} is the design matrix with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {rank}(\text {A}) = n$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {x} \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document} is the vector of unknown parameters, and k-alternative hypotheses with index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{1,...,k\}$$\end{document}. The outliers in the observation model are specified by the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {C}_i \in \mathbb {R}^{m \times q_i}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {rank}([\text {A},\text {C}_i]) = n+q_i$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_i \in \mathbb {R}^{q_i}$$\end{document} being the (unknown) vector containing the sizes of the outliers. The misclosure vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document}, which has the dimension of the redundancy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r = m - n$$\end{document}, can be used to build test statistics for the decision problem in (3), since it provides a measure of inconsistency between the model under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document} and the observables. The expressions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document} and its vc-matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}} \in \mathbb {R}^{r \times r}$$\end{document} are Teunissen (2006)4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \underline{\text {t}} = \text {B}^T \underline{\text {y}},~~~\text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}} = \text {B}^T \text {Q}_{\text {y} \text {y}} \text {B}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {B}\in \mathbb {R}^{m \times r}$$\end{document} is a basis matrix of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}(\text {A})^{\perp }$$\end{document} (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {B}^T\text {A} = 0_{r \times n}$$\end{document}). The misclosure vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document} can be used to connect the Best Linear Unbiased Estimators (BLUEs) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} The BLUE-inverse of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {C}_{\text {t}_i} = \text {B}^T \text {C}_i$$\end{document} is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {C}^+_{\text {t}_i} = (\text {C}_{\text {t}_i}^T \text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}^{-1}\text {C}_{\text {t}_i})^{-1}\text {C}^T_{\text {t}_i}\text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}^{-1}$$\end{document}. The PDF of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left[ \hat{\underline{\text {x}}}^T_i~\underline{\text {t}}^T\right] ^T$$\end{document}, under hypothesis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_a$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in \{0,...,k\}$$\end{document}, is6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {H}_a: \begin{bmatrix} \hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_i\\ \underline{\text {t}}\\ \end{bmatrix} \sim \mathcal {N} \left( \begin{bmatrix} \text {x} + \text {A}^+\text {R}_i \text {C}_a \text {b}_a\\ \text {B}^T \text {C}_a \text {b}_a \end{bmatrix},~ \begin{bmatrix} \text {Q}_{\hat{\text {x}}_0 \hat{\text {x}}_0} + \text {L}_i \text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}} \text {L}_i^T & -\text {L}_i \text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}\\ -\text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}\text {L}_i^T & \text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}} \end{bmatrix} \right) ~, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {R}_i = \text {I}_m - \text {C}_i \left( \text {B}^T \text {C}_i\right) ^{+}\text {B}^T$$\end{document}, in which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \text {B}^T \text {C}_i\right) ^{+} = \left( \text {C}^T_i \text {B}\,\text {Q}^{-1}_{\text {t} \text {t}} \text {B}^T\text {C}_i \right) ^{-1}\text {C}^T_i \text {B}\,\text {Q}^{-1}_{\text {t} \text {t}}$$\end{document}. The matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {R}_i$$\end{document} projects along \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}(\text {C}_i)$$\end{document} and onto \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}(\text {A},\text {Q}_{\text {y} \text {y}}\text {B}(\text {B}^T\text {C}_i)^{\perp })$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\text {B}^T\text {C}_i)^{\perp }$$\end{document} being a basis matrix of the null space of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {C}^T_i \text {B}$$\end{document}. The variance-covariance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Q}_{\hat{\text {x}}_0 \hat{\text {x}}_0} = (\text {A}^T \text {Q}_{\text {y} \text {y}}^{-1} \text {A})^{-1}$$\end{document} is the one of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_0$$\end{document}. Note that for a given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_a$$\end{document}, the following two-cases hold for the joint PDF of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left[ \hat{\underline{\text {x}}}^T_i~\underline{\text {t}}^T\right] ^T$$\end{document},7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f_{\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_i, \underline{\text {t}}}(\textit{x},\textit{t}) {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} = f_{\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_0}(\textit{x})f_{\underline{\text {t}}}(\textit{t}) & ,~\text {if}~i=0\\ \ne f_{\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_i}(\textit{x})f_{\underline{\text {t}}}(\textit{t}) & ,~\text {if}~i\ne 0, \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}which shows that, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\ne 0$$\end{document}, the BLUEs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_i$$\end{document} and the misclosure vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}}$$\end{document} are dependent (see an illustrative example in Section IV.1 of Ciuban et al. (2024)). By applying partitioning principles, the hypothesis testing problem in (3) can be represented in the misclosure vector space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document}. Partitions in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document} can be formulated based on the subsets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_i \subset \mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document}, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{0,...,k\}$$\end{document}, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cup ^k_{i=0} \mathcal {P}_i = \mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_i \cap \mathcal {P}_j = \{ 0 \}$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \ne j$$\end{document}. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k+1$$\end{document} partitions can be defined as follows,8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \mathcal {P}_0&= \left\{ \textit{t} \in \mathbb {R}^r~|~\vert \vert \textit{t} \vert \vert ^2_{\text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}} \le \chi ^2_{\alpha }(r,0) \right\} , \\ \mathcal {P}_{i \ne 0}&= \left\{ \textit{t} \in \mathbb {R}^r~|~\textit{t} \notin \mathcal {P}_0,~\check{\textsf{T}}_{i}=\underset{l \in \{1,...,k\}}{\max } ~\textsf{T}_l \right\} , \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$||\underline{\text {t}}||^2_{\text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}}$$\end{document} is the overall model test statistic, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi ^2_{\alpha }(r,0)$$\end{document} is the Chi-squared critical value for a level of significance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{T}}_l$$\end{document} is the result of the following transformation (Teunissen 2017; Zaminpardaz and Teunissen 2023)9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \underline{\textsf{T}}_l = \text {CDF}_{\chi ^2(q_l,0)}\left( \vert \vert \mathbf {\Pi }_{\text {C}_{\text {t}_l}} \underline{\text {t}} \vert \vert ^2_{\text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}}\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {CDF}_{\chi ^2(q_l,0)}(.)$$\end{document} is the cumulative distribution function (CDF) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi ^2(q_l,0)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vert \vert \mathbf {\Pi }_{\text {C}_{\text {t}_l}} \underline{\text {t}} \vert \vert ^2_{\text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}} \overset{\mathcal {H}_0}{\sim } \chi ^2(q_l,0)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf {\Pi }_{\text {C}_{\text {t}_l}} = \text {C}_{\text {t}_l} \text {C}^+_{\text {t}_l}$$\end{document} projects onto \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}(\text {C}_{\text {t}_l})$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {C}_{\text {t}_l} = \text {B}^T \text {C}_l$$\end{document} while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {C}^+_{\text {t}_l} = (\text {C}_{\text {t}_l}^T \text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}^{-1}\text {C}_{\text {t}_l})^{-1}\text {C}^T_{\text {t}_l}\text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}^{-1}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{T}}_l \overset{\mathcal {H}_0}{\sim } \mathcal {U}(0,1)$$\end{document}. Note that the evaluation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {CDF}_{\chi ^2(q_l,0)}(.)$$\end{document} must be done k-times, which can be computationally expensive for a larger number of alternative hypothesis. To mitigate this, one can: (i) group the alternative hypotheses by their common dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{l}$$\end{document}, and denote the number of resulting groups as g; for a simple example with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m = 4$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r=3$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=10$$\end{document}, we have four one-dimensional outliers, one per observation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{l \in \{1,...,4\}} = 1$$\end{document} and six two-dimensional ones \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{l \in \{5,...,10\}} = 2$$\end{document}, and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g = 2$$\end{document}; (ii) within each group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}$$\end{document}, compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underset{l \in \mathcal {G}}{\max } ~\vert \vert \mathbf {\Pi }_{\text {C}_{\text {t}_l}} \underline{\text {t}} \vert \vert ^2_{\text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}}$$\end{document}; (iii) evaluate the corresponding CDF at each of these maximum values; (iv) select the maximum among these g transformed CDF values. This procedure reduces the evaluation of the CDF from k-times to g-times.

The role of the partitions in (8) is such that a hypothesis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_i$$\end{document}, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{0,...,k\}$$\end{document}, is selected as the most likely one if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_i$$\end{document}. (c) partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_{i \ne 0}$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{1,2,5\}$$\end{document} while the white space represents \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^{r=3}\backslash (\cup _{i \in \{0,1,2,5\}} \mathcal {P}_i)$$\end{document}

Note that an undecided region \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subset \mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document} can be included to accommodate situations when it would be difficult to discriminate between hypotheses or when not all possible outliers can be anticipated. In this contribution, we consider that always an estimator is selected (i.e., no \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \subset \mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document} is included). The decision outcome in statistical hypothesis testing is determined by where the misclosure vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}}$$\end{document} lands in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document} with partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_i \subset \mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document}, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{0,..,k\}$$\end{document}, cf. (8). Under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document}, the decision results are: (i) Correct Acceptance (CA) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document} when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_0$$\end{document}, and (ii) False Alarm (FA) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \notin \mathcal {P}_0$$\end{document}, or specifically the FA per alternative hypothesis (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {FA}_i$$\end{document}) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_i$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i>0$$\end{document}. The probabilities of these decisions are given by10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textsf{P}_{\text {CA}}= \textsf{P}\left( \underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_0|\mathcal {H}_0 \right) ,~~ \textsf{P}_{\text {FA}}= \sum ^k_{i=1} \underbrace{\textsf{P}\left( \underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_i|\mathcal {H}_0 \right) }_{\textsf{P}_{\text {FA}_i}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{P}_{\text {FA}} = \alpha $$\end{document} is the level of significance and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{P}_{\text {CA}} + \textsf{P}_{\text {FA}} = 1$$\end{document}. Similarly, under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_i$$\end{document}, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i > 0$$\end{document}, the outcomes of the decisions are: (i) missed detection (MD) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_0$$\end{document}, (ii) Correct Identification (CI) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_i$$\end{document}, and (iii) Wrong Identification (WI) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_j$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\notin \{0,i\}$$\end{document}. Their probabilities are11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\textsf{P}_{\text {MD}_i} = \textsf{P}\left( \underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_0|\mathcal {H}_i \right) ,~~\textsf{P}_{\text {CI}_i} = \textsf{P}\left( \underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_i|\mathcal {H}_i \right) ,\\&\textsf{P}_{\text {WI}} = \sum ^k_{j \ne 0,i} \underbrace{\textsf{P}\left( \underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_j|\mathcal {H}_i \right) }_{\textsf{P}_{\text {WI}_j}}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where the decision outcome of Correct Detection (CD) is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{P}_{\text {CD}_i} = \textsf{P}_{\text {CI}_i} + \textsf{P}_{\text {WI}}$$\end{document} satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{P}_{\text {MD}_i} + \textsf{P}_{\text {CD}_i} = 1$$\end{document}. The probabilities in (11) depend on the unknown vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_i \in \mathbb {R}^{q_i}$$\end{document} since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\underline{\text {t}}}(t|\mathcal {H}_i) = \mathcal {N}\left( \text {B}^T \text {C}_i \text {b}_i, \text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}\right) $$\end{document}.

The Detection (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\texttt{D}$$\end{document}) Identification (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\texttt{I}$$\end{document}) and Adaptation (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\texttt{A}$$\end{document}) statistical testing procedure is then12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \text {if}~\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_0~{\text {(no}~\texttt{D})} \rightarrow ~\text {output}~\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_0,\\ \text {if}~\underline{\text {t}} \notin \mathcal {P}_0~{(\texttt{D})} \rightarrow ~\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_{i \ne 0}~{(\texttt{I})} \rightarrow ~\text {output}~\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_i~{(\texttt{A})},\\ \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_0 \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_i \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document} are the Best Linear Unbiased Estimators (BLUEs) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {x} \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document} under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{i \ne 0}$$\end{document}. The estimator that captures the procedure in (12) is the DIA-estimator (Teunissen 2018)13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{\underline{\text {x}}} = \sum ^k_{i=0} \hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_i\, p_i(\underline{\text {t}}) = \hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_0 - \sum ^k_{i=1} \text {L}_i\,\underline{\text {t}}\, p_i(\underline{\text {t}}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where the indicator function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_i(\underline{\text {t}}) = 1$$\end{document} if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_i$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_i(\underline{\text {t}}) = 0$$\end{document} otherwise. The uncertainty of parameter estimation is carried by the BLUEs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_i$$\end{document} and uncertainty of statistical testing is carried by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_i(\underline{\text {t}})$$\end{document}. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_i(\underline{\text {t}})$$\end{document} is a nonlinear function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}}$$\end{document}, which causes the PDF of the DIA-estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\overline{\text {x}}}$$\end{document} to be multimodal, even though the individual PDFs of the BLUEs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_i$$\end{document} are Gaussian. The PDF of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\underline{\text {x}}} \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document} follows from Theorem 1 in Teunissen (2018)14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f_{\overline{\underline{\text {x}} }}\left( \textit{x}\right) = \sum _{i=0}^{k} \int _{\mathcal {P}_i} f_{\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_i,\underline{\text {t}}}\left( \textit{x},\textit{t} \right) d\textit{t}~. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The conditional components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\overline{\underline{\text {x}} }}\left( \textit{x}\right) $$\end{document} on the testing decisions under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document} (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {CA}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {FA}_i$$\end{document}) can be obtained from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\overline{\underline{\text {x}} }}\left( \textit{x} |\mathcal {H}_0\right) $$\end{document}. Those corresponding to the testing decisions under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_i$$\end{document} (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {MD}_i$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {CI}_i, \text {WI}_j$$\end{document}) from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\overline{\underline{\text {x}} }}\left( \textit{x} |\mathcal {H}_i\right) $$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\ne 0$$\end{document}. It is the DIA-estimator’s PDF in (14) that is used in the formulation of the probability of positioning failure. We note that one may only be interested in specific components of the parameter vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {x} \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}, such as the 1D, 2D, or 3D position. The desired position components can be obtained through the linear transformation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {h} = \text {H}^T \text {x}$$\end{document} with an appropriately chosen \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {H} \in \mathbb {R}^{n \times p}$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p < n$$\end{document}. Further developments in this paper are done in terms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {x} \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}, however a similar approach would apply also for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {h} \in \mathbb {R}^p$$\end{document}.

Probability of positioning failure and its components

The positioning failure event is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F} = \overline{\underline{\text {x}}} \in \mathcal {B}^c$$\end{document}, according to page 15 of Special Committee 159, R.T.C.A. (2020), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B} \subset \mathbb {R}^{n}$$\end{document} denotes the safety region, and its complement \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}^c = \mathbb {R}^n \setminus \mathcal {B}$$\end{document} represents the failure region. We now proceed to formulate the probability of positioning failure15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}} (\textbf{b}) = \int _{\mathcal {B}^c} f_{\overline{\underline{\text {x}}}}(x)d x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{b} = \{\text {b}_1 \in \mathbb {R}^{q_1},\text {b}_2 \in \mathbb {R}^{q_2},...,\text {b}_k \in \mathbb {R}^{q_k}\}$$\end{document} represents the vectors of the outliers in the observation model under the k-alternative hypotheses. In general, the dimensions of the model outliers may differ, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i \ne q_j$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \ne j$$\end{document}. The next step is to decompose (15) via the rule of total probability16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}} (\textbf{b}) = \textsf{P}(\mathcal {H}_0)&\underbrace{\int _{\mathcal {B}^c} f_{\overline{\underline{\text {x}}}}(\textit{x}|\mathcal {H}_0) dx}_{\mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}}|\mathcal {H}_0 } + \sum ^k_{i=1}\textsf{P}(\mathcal {H}_i) \underbrace{\int _{\mathcal {B}^c} f_{\overline{\underline{\text {x}}}}(\textit{x}|\mathcal {H}_i) dx}_{\mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}}|\mathcal {H}_i(\text {b}_i)}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{P}(\mathcal {H}_0)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{P}(\mathcal {H}_i)$$\end{document} are the apriori probability of occurrence of the hypotheses. A further expansion of (16), based on (14), gives17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}} (\textbf{b}) = \textsf{P}&(\mathcal {H}_0) \left( \sum ^k_{j=0} \int _{\mathcal {B}^c} \int _{\mathcal {P}_j} f_{\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_j,\underline{\text {t}}}\left( x,t|\mathcal {H}_0\right) dt\,dx \right) + \\&~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\sum _{i=1}^{k} \textsf{P}(\mathcal {H}_i) \left( \sum ^k_{j=0} \int _{\mathcal {B}^c} \int _{\mathcal {P}_j} f_{\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_j,\underline{\text {t}}}\left( x,t|\mathcal {H}_i \right) dt\,dx \right) , \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}which can then be expressed in terms of expected values w.r.t. the joint PDFs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_j,\underline{\text {t}}}\left( \textit{x},\textit{t} \right) $$\end{document}18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} The two summations of joint probabilities in parentheses in (18) are rewritten below, explicitly incorporating the statistical testing decisions from (10)-(11) into the notation,19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \sum ^k_{j=0} \textsf{E}_{f_{\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_j,\underline{\text {t}}}}\left( 1\!\!1_j(\underline{\text {x}}, \underline{\text {t}} )|\mathcal {H}_0 \right)&= \mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}}|\text {CA}\,\textsf{P}_{\text {CA}} +\sum _{j=1}^{k}\mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}}|\text {FA}_j\,\textsf{P}_{\text {FA}_j}, \\ \sum ^k_{j=0} \textsf{E}_{f_{\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_j,\underline{\text {t}}}}\left( 1\!\!1_j(\underline{\text {x}}, \underline{\text {t}} )|\mathcal {H}_i \right)&= \mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}}|\text {MD}_i\,\textsf{P}_{\text {MD}_i}+ \mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}}|\text {CI}_i\,\textsf{P}_{\text {CI}_i} + \sum _{j \ne 0,i}^{k}\mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}}|\text {WI}_j\,\textsf{P}_{\text {WI}_{j}}~. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}}|\mathcal {E}$$\end{document} is the probability of positioning failure conditioned on the testing decision \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}\in \{\text {CA},\,\text {FA}_j,\,\text {MD}_i,\,\text {CI}_i,\,\text {WI}_{j}\}$$\end{document}. These decompositions are schematically illustrated as a ’failure-tree’ in Fig. 2, where Level 1 corresponds to (19) and Level 2 corresponds to (16). To obtain the Level 1 and Level 2 components depicted in Fig. 2, one can resort to Monte Carlo simulation to approximate the expected values in (18). However, if these components are on the order of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-4}$$\end{document} or less, the variance of the results of the standard Monte Carlo simulation may be too high or the probability may not be even computable (Rubinstein and Kroese 2008). We have addressed these challenges in recent work (Ciuban et al. 2025b), where we developed a method that enables the construction of the ’failure-tree’ in Fig. 2 by leveraging principles from rare event simulation–an approach also applied in this contribution. We give the three main steps of the proposed method below: Fig. 2Representation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}}(\textbf{b})$$\end{document} as a ’failure-tree’. Level 1 corresponds to (19) and Level 2 to (16)

Level 1 corresponds to (19) and Level 2 to (16)
