The uncertainty in the computation of the expected values in (18) is due to the use of simulations. Figure 7(a) shows that the component corresponding to the CA testing decision (accepting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document} while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document} is valid) has a mean value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{\text {sim}}$$\end{document} of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2.1872 \cdot 10^{-11}$$\end{document} across the 50 simulations. In contrast, the sum component related to all of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {FA}_i$$\end{document} testing decisions (accepting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{i \ne 0}$$\end{document} while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document} is valid) has a mean value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{\text {sim}}$$\end{document} of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$9.3056 \cdot 10^{-5}$$\end{document}, which not only represents the dominant contribution to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document}, but is also approximately seven orders of magnitude larger than than the component corresponding to the CA testing decision. If the summation in (31) is split into two parts–one corresponding to alternative hypotheses involving one-dimensional outliers (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i=1$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{1,..,8 \}$$\end{document}) and the other to those involving all the two-dimensional outliers (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i = 2$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \{9,...,36\}$$\end{document}) –then the latter is shown in Fig. 7(b) to contribute most significantly to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document}. This is expected since the components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {FA}_i}\,f_{\overline{\underline{\text {h}} } |\text {FA}_i}\left( h | \text {FA}_i \right) $$\end{document}, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i =2$$\end{document}, tend to exhibit ’quad’-modal behavior with a greater portion of their density lying outside the safety region–already accounted for by the ’weighting’ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {FA}_i}$$\end{document}. The contribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum ^{k=36}_{i=9} \underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {FA}_i} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_i$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document} is approximately one order of magnitude larger than the one of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum ^{8}_{i=1} \underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {FA}_i} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_i$$\end{document}. Taking a closer look at the two summation term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum ^{8}_{i=1} \underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {FA}_i} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_i$$\end{document}, Fig. 7(c) shows that the component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {FA}_2} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_2$$\end{document} is the largest among the group with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i = 1$$\end{document}, which is consistent with the fact that GPS satellite 2 is the only one located in the azimuth range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0^{\circ }-90^{\circ }$$\end{document} (see Fig. 3). In the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum ^{k=36}_{i=9} \underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {FA}_i} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_i$$\end{document}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i = 2$$\end{document}, the components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {FA}_{16}} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_{16}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {FA}_{18}} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_{18}$$\end{document} are most dominant - not only within that group, but also among all components with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \{1,...,36\}$$\end{document}. Results are obtained over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\text {sim}} = 50$$\end{document}, from which the mean values (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{\text {sim}}$$\end{document}) and their standard deviation (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _{\text {sim}}$$\end{document}) were computed

Results under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{i\ne 0}$$\end{document}

Under the alternative hypotheses, the probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{i}(\text {b}_{i})$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\ne 0$$\end{document} depends on the vector containing the sizes of the outliers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_{i} \in \mathbb {R}^{q_i}$$\end{document}. The objective is to find its maximum (worst-case scenario). As a first example, we express the computed Level 1 components corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{7}$$\end{document} (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{7}(\text {b}_{7}) = \textsf{P}_{\text {MD}_{7}}\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {MD}_{7} + \underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {CI}_{7}}\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {CI}_{7} + \sum ^{k=36}_{j \ne 0,7} \underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {WI}_j}\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {WI}_j, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{P}_{\text {MD}_{7}} = \textsf{P}\left( \underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_0\,| \mathcal {H}_7\right) $$\end{document} can be computed exactly for any value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_{7}$$\end{document}. One contributing factor to the dominant behavior of the three components in Fig. 8(c) is the angles between the subspaces spanned by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}\left( \text {C}_{\text {t}_j} \right) $$\end{document}, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\in \{15, 28, 31\}$$\end{document}, and the subspace spanned by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}\left( \text {c}_{\text {t}_7} \right) $$\end{document} in the misclosure space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^{r=4}$$\end{document}. The smaller these angles are, the closer the corresponding subspaces are (see Table 3 and Appendix 8 for all the other angles), and hence the larger the probabilities of wrong identifications. \right) $$\end{document} in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_7$$\end{document} (as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_7$$\end{document} increases), the probability density outside \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_7$$\end{document} is also increasing in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_7$$\end{document} reaches approximately 8.4 [m], the faster decreasing behavior of the density in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} The second contributing factor is how the probability density of conditional PDFs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\overline{\underline{\text {h}} }| \text {WI}_{j}}\left( h | \text {WI}_{j}\right) $$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\in \{15,28,31 \}$$\end{document} varies in relation to the safety region as a function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_7$$\end{document}. For example, if the satellite pair 1–8 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{15}$$\end{document}) is wrongfully excluded while the outlier is in satellite 7, leads to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\overline{\underline{\text {h}} }| \text {WI}_{7}}\left( h | \text {WI}_{7}\right) $$\end{document} having a high density outside \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}_{\text {h}}$$\end{document} in the horizontal direction corresponding to the skyplot azimuth range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$270^{\circ } - 0^{\circ }$$\end{document} (see Fig. 3). Hence, the dominant behavior of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {WI}_{15}}\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {WI}_{15}$$\end{document} is initially driven by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {WI}_{15}$$\end{document} up to its peak around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_7 = 8.4$$\end{document} [m]. Beyond that point, the behavior is governed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {WI}_{15}}$$\end{document} which decreases more rapidly than the other components in the plot as the angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\measuredangle \left( \mathcal {R}\left( \text {c}_{\text {t}_7} \right) , \mathcal {R}\left( \text {C}_{\text {t}_{15}} \right) \right) = 31.06^{\circ }$$\end{document} is larger than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\measuredangle \left( \mathcal {R}\left( \text {c}_{\text {t}_7} \right) , \mathcal {R}\left( \text {C}_{\text {t}_{28}} \right) \right) = 12.32^{\circ }$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\measuredangle \left( \mathcal {R}\left( \text {c}_{\text {t}_7} \right) , \mathcal {R}\left( \text {C}_{\text {t}_{31}} \right) \right) = 13.80^{\circ }$$\end{document} (see Table 3). A similar reasoning can be extended to the remaining components. Therefore, wrongfully excluding these groups of satellites–while the actual outlier is present in GPS satellite 7–leads the corresponding terms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {WI}_{j}}\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {WI}_{j}$$\end{document}, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\in \{15,28,31\}$$\end{document}, to dominate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{7}(\text {b}_{7})$$\end{document} for outlier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_7$$\end{document} values around 8 and 21 [m]. An interesting point to note is that the global maximum of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{7}(\text {b}_{7})$$\end{document} arises from the combined contribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {WI}_{28}}\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {WI}_{28}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {WI}_{31}}\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {WI}_{31}$$\end{document}.

As a second example, we analyze the probabilities of positioning failure under several hypotheses where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i = 2$$\end{document}, such as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_9$$\end{document} (outliers in GPS Satellites 1 and 2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{16}$$\end{document} (outliers in GPS Satellites 2 and 3), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{17}$$\end{document} (outliers in GPS Satellites 2 and 4). The corresponding probabilities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{i}(\text {b}_{i})$$\end{document}, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{9,16,17\}$$\end{document}, are displayed on two-dimensional grids in Fig. 10. These grids were obtained based on the following parametrization (Teunissen 2006)33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {b}_i = \sqrt{\dfrac{\lambda ^2_0}{|| \Pi ^{\perp }_{\text {A}} \text {C}_i\, \text {d}||^2_{\text {Q}_{\text {y} \text {y}}}}}\,\text {d} = \sqrt{\dfrac{\lambda ^2_0}{|| \text {C}_{\text {t}_i}\, \text {d}||^2_{\text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}}}}\,\text {d} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^2_0$$\end{document} is the non-centrality parameter of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vert \vert \underline{\text {t}} \vert \vert ^2_{\text {Q}_{\text {t} \text {t}}} \overset{\mathcal {H}_i}{\sim } \chi ^2(r,\lambda ^2_0)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {d} \in \mathbb {R}^{q_i = 2}$$\end{document} is a unit vector which scans the unit circle in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^{q_i=2}$$\end{document}. As a result, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_i$$\end{document} scans an ellipse in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^{q_i=2}$$\end{document} as described by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^2_0 = || \Pi ^{\perp }_{\text {A}} \text {C}_i\, \text {b}_i||^2_{\text {Q}_{\text {y} \text {y}}} $$\end{document} (see Teunissen (2006) at page 105 and Fig. 10). The non-centrality parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^2_0$$\end{document} is selected such that the corresponding probability of correct detection is high (i.e., close to 1), ensuring that the ellipse encompasses a representative set of vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_i$$\end{document}. The area within these ellipses is then discretized to form 2D grids of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_i$$\end{document}, over which the probabilities are computed. In all three plots in Fig. 10, the probabilities are highest (dot colors ranging from light blue to yellow) in situations where there is an approximately one-to-one relationship between the components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_i$$\end{document}’s, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{9,16,17\}$$\end{document}. In contrast, the probabilities are lowest (dot colors in dark blue) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_2$$\end{document} lies approximately in the range of –10 to 10 [m], while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_3$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_{4}$$\end{document} vary across their entire respective ranges. Figure 10(a) illustrates the shape and orientation of the grid for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_{9} = [\text {b}_2~\text {b}_1]^T \in \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}, corresponding to the exclusion of GPS satellites 2 and 1 from the positioning model in (21). Figure 10(b) shows that the grid of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_{16} = [\text {b}_2~\text {b}_3]^T \in \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document} has the largest elongation in the North-East direction as GPS satellite 2 and 3 are the only ones in the azimuth range of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0^{\circ }$$\end{document}-\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$120^{\circ }$$\end{document} (see Fig. 3). In the case of Fig. 10(c), the grid of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_{17} = [\text {b}_2~\text {b}_4]^T \in \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document} is closer to a circular shape, as the elongation caused by the exclusion from the positioning model of satellites 2 and 4 is less pronounced. The maximum value for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{9}(\text {b}_{9})$$\end{document} is attained at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4.8613 \cdot 10^{-1}$$\end{document} (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _{\text {sim}} = 0.0047 \cdot 10^{-1}$$\end{document}) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm \text {b}_9 = [15.79~13.70]^T~[\text {m}]$$\end{document}. In the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{16}(\text {b}_{16})$$\end{document} (Fig. 10(b)) the maximum is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$9.1177 \cdot 10^{-1}$$\end{document} (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _{\text {sim}} = 0.0053 \cdot 10^{-1}$$\end{document}) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm \text {b}_{16} = [21.04~14.68]^T~[\text {m}]$$\end{document}, and that of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{17}(\text {b}_{17})$$\end{document} (Fig. 10(c)) the maximum is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$5.9342 \cdot 10^{-1}$$\end{document} (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _{\text {sim}} = 0.0050 \cdot 10^{-1}$$\end{document}) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm \text {b}_{17} = [15.35~-24.90]^T~[\text {m}]$$\end{document}. The maximum is determined in a similar manner for the other cases of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{i}(\text {b}_{i})$$\end{document}, for

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \notin \{0,9,16,17\}$$\end{document}. The maximum values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{i}(\text {b}_{i})$$\end{document} for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{1,...,36\}$$\end{document} are shown in Fig. 11. Figure 11 shows that the maximum values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{i}(\text {b}_{i})$$\end{document} among the components corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i = 1$$\end{document} (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \{1,...,8\})$$\end{document} are highest for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{2}(\text {b}_{2})$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{3}(\text {b}_{3})$$\end{document}. This is expected, as one-dimensional outliers at a time in GPS satellites 2 and 3–the only ones located in the azimuth range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0^{\circ }-120^{\circ }$$\end{document} of the skyplot–have a greater impact on horizontal positioning performance. Since GPS satellites 2 and 3 are the only ones in the aforementioned azimuth range, outliers in their measurements have a stronger influence on the horizontal solution due to the receiver–satellite geometry. Furthermore, the maximum values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{i}(\text {b}_{i})$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i = 2$$\end{document} (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \{9,...,36\})$$\end{document} are several times larger than those for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i = 1$$\end{document} (i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{1,..,8\}$$\end{document}). The largest value among all alternative hypotheses corresponds to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{16}(\text {b}_{16})$$\end{document}, which is associated with the simultaneous two-dimensional outliers in the pseudoranges from GPS satellites 2 and 3 (see Fig. 3).

Ellipses shown with dashed lines are defined by (33), the interiors of which are then discretized; (a) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{9}(\text {b}_{9})$$\end{document} over a grid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_9 = [\text {b}_2~\text {b}_1]^T~\in \mathbb {R}^{q_9=2}~[\text {m}]$$\end{document}; (b) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{16}(\text {b}_{16})$$\end{document} over a grid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_{16} = [\text {b}_2~\text {b}_3]^T~\in \mathbb {R}^{q_{16}=2}~[\text {m}]$$\end{document}; (c) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{17}(\text {b}_{17})$$\end{document} over a grid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_{17} = [\text {b}_2~\text {b}_4]^T~\in \mathbb {R}^{q_{17}=2}~[\text {m}]$$\end{document}

Maximum values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{i}(\text {b}_{i})$$\end{document} for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{1,...,36\}$$\end{document}. The red stem represents \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{16}(\text {b}_{16})$$\end{document}, which corresponds to two-dimensional outliers in the pseudoranges from GPS Satellites 2 and 3

If we set the level of significance to a much lower value, for example \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = 10^{-5}$$\end{document}, the results indicate that only Case 3 (the most optimistic) would be appropriate for SAIL 4. This is because the contribution of false alarm components to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}} | \mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document} is lower when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = 10^{-5}$$\end{document}, and also because the a priori probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{P}(\mathcal {H}_0)$$\end{document} is high for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi = 10^{-5}$$\end{document}, resulting in a greater contribution to (34). However, caution should be exercised when relying too heavily on the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} Since these results correspond to a receiver–GPS satellite geometry at a single instant of time (see Fig. 3), the next step is to investigate the results obtained over a 24-hour period, accounting for the evolution of GPS satellite positions in their orbits.

GPS satellite geometry over 24 h period

In Fig. 12(a) we show the number of observed GPS satellites (in black), using a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{\circ }$$\end{document} elevation mask, and the weighted Horizontal Dilution of Precision (wHDOP) in orange, which is computed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {wHDOP} = \sqrt{\text {trace}\left( \text {H}^T \left( \text {A}^T \text {Q}^{-1}_{\text {y} \text {y}}\text {A}\right) ^{-1}\text {H} \right) }~[\text {m}]$$\end{document} based on Won (2012). The wHDOP is useful for giving an indication of the variation of the horizontal positioning precision as a function of the change in GPS satellite geometry while also taking into account the elevation-dependent weighting of the observables. Figure 12(b) shows the total number of alternative hypotheses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_{i \ne 0}$$\end{document}, including both one-dimensional (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i=1$$\end{document}) and two-dimensional (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i=2$$\end{document}) outliers. It can be seen that the total number of hypotheses (k) varies between 21 and 66. The components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_{0}$$\end{document} are shown in Fig. 12(c), where the dominant component is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum ^{k}_{i=1} \underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {FA}_i} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_i$$\end{document} except at 3 h and 25 min (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\approx $$\end{document} 3.4 h) and at 6 h and 30 min (6.5 h), when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{P}_{\text {CA}} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {CA}$$\end{document} domaintes due to poor wHDOP. Maximum values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\breve{\underline{\mathbb {P}}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}} (\breve{\textbf{b}})$$\end{document} for the highlighted seven points in Fig. 12. In the current plot these points are indexed from 1 to 7, left to right; (a) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = 10^{-3}$$\end{document}; (b) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = 10^{-5}$$\end{document}

If the requirements and guidelines are not satisfied, then appropriate changes to the measurement setup (functional and stochastic models), safety-region, or the combined parameter estimation and statistical hypothesis testing procedure, may be necessary. For instance, the new theoretical framework introduced in Teunissen (2024) shows how fit-for-purpose statistical hypothesis testing can improve the performance of DIA-estimators in terms of positioning safety.

Computational resources

The computations in this work were performed using MATLAB R2023b with the Parallel Computing Toolbox on the Snellius Supercomputer, provided by the Dutch National e-Infrastructure. As an illustration of the computational load, evaluating the components (32) to obtain the results in Figs. 8 required computation times on the order of several minutes. These times should be seen as indicative, since they depend on the implementation details, available hardware, and programming environment.

Summary and concluding remarks

In this contribution, we presented a general approach for performing safety analyses–such as positioning safety–based on the DIA-estimator, while accounting for multidimensional outliers in the observation model. Specifically, the study focused on misspecifications in the mean of the observation model–such as those caused by outliers in the observations. The approach includes: (i) use of the DIA-estimator and of its PDF to account for the combined uncertainty of estimation and hypothesis testing (Teunissen 2018; ii) formulation of the probability of positioning failure and of its components for an application dependent safety-region (Ciuban et al. 2025a, 2024; iii) computation of the probability of positioning failure and of its conditional components via a recently developed method (Ciuban et al. 2025b). This approach is consistent with the scenario-based safety assessment framework, which is commonly used in domains such as automated and autonomous vehicles (Riedmaier 2020; United Nations Economic Commission for Europe 2023; Gelder 2024) and UAVs (Khatiri 2023), among others.

As an illustrative case, we conducted a positioning safety analysis for a UAV, considering two scenarios involving one- and two-dimensional outlier misspecifications in the pseudorange measurements. The choice of the positioning model for the UAV, under nominal conditions, was based on the TSO certification for GPS-based UAV positioning (FAA 2017; Cozzens 2021). In the first scenario, we considered a fixed receiver-satellite geometry at a single snapshot of time (see Fig. 3) to study the shape of the DIA-estimator PDF and to gain insights into the components that contribute the most to the probability of positioning failure under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document}. For example, the conditional components related to the False Alarm (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {FA}_i$$\end{document}) testing decisions of the DIA-estimator’s PDF (see Fig. 5) exhibited bimodal and quadrimodal structures (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\overline{\underline{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_2}(h|\text {FA}_2)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\overline{\underline{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_{20}}(h|\text {FA}_{20})$$\end{document}). The number of modes of a component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\overline{\underline{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_i}(h|\text {FA}_i) = \textsf{E}_{f_{\underline{\text {t}}}}\left[ f_{\hat{\underline{\text {h}}}_0}(h + \text {G}_i \text {C}^+_{\text {t}_i} \underline{\text {t}}\,|\mathcal {H}_0 )p_i(\underline{\text {t}})\right] /{\textsf{P}_{\text {FA}_i}}$$\end{document} depends on: (i) the number of columns of the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {G}_i \in \mathbb {R}^{2 \times q_i}$$\end{document}, and hence the dimension of the outlier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_i$$\end{document}, and (ii) on the linear combinations of the columns of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {G}_i \in \mathbb {R}^{2 \times q_i}$$\end{document}, weighted by the coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \text {C}^+_{\text {t}_i}\underline{\text {t}}\right) $$\end{document}, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathcal {P}_i$$\end{document} (see Fig. 6). Notably, point (ii) depends on the hypothesis testing procedure, specifically on the partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_i \subset \mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document} of the misclosure vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document}, meaning that different partitions (e.g., Teunissen 2024) can lead to different number of modes and shapes for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_{\overline{\underline{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_i}(h|\text {FA}_i)$$\end{document}.

The positioning safety analysis under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_7$$\end{document} (one-dimensional outlier in GPS satellite 7) revealed that the probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_7(\text {b}_7)$$\end{document} has a local and global maximum (see Fig. 8). These maxima were caused by the components conditioned on the Wrongful Identification (WI) testing decision \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {WI}_{15}} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {WI}_{15}$$\end{document} (pair 1–8), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {WI}_{28}} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {WI}_{28}$$\end{document} (pair 4–6), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {WI}_{31}} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {WI}_{31}$$\end{document} (pair 5–6). The primary reason these components contributed the most is the strong correlation between the corresponding test statistics, combined with the degraded satellite geometry caused by the wrongful exclusion of these satellite pairs while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_7$$\end{document} was valid. In general, the probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_i(\text {b}_i)$$\end{document} can exhibit multiple local maxima as a function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_i \in \mathbb {R}^{q_i}$$\end{document}. These components can be revealed through a component-wise analysis, as carried out in this contribution and in Ciuban et al. (2024, 2025a, 2025b).

In the second scenario we have considered a 24-hours (on May 24, 2024) evolution of GPS satellites moving over an airspace region in The Netherlands for which authorization can be obtained for UAV operations (see Fig. 12). The total number of alternative hypotheses (k) that account for both one- and two-dimensional outliers varied between 21 and 66. Under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document}, the computed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document} was mainly driven by the component that accounts for all false alarm testing decisions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum ^{k}_{i=1} \underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {FA}_i} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_i$$\end{document}. There were two cases in which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {CA}} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {CA} > \sum ^{k}_{i=1} \underline{\textsf{P}}_{\text {FA}_i} \underline{\mathbb {P}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}|\text {FA}_i$$\end{document} (see Fig. 12(c)), both corresponding to the weakest satellite geometries over the 24 h, characterized by wHDOP values of 3.4 and 4.1. The results of the worst-case scenarios (w.r.t. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {b}_i \in \mathbb {R}^{q_i}$$\end{document}) for the selected points (see Fig. 12(d)) showed that Case 3 (when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi =10^{-5}$$\end{document} in Table 4) is consistently below \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-5}$$\end{document} (order of SAIL 4 requirement en-route operations). However, caution should be exercised when relying too heavily on the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_0$$\end{document} component of the maximum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\breve{\underline{\mathbb {P}}}_{\mathcal {F}_{\text {h}}}(\breve{\textbf{b}})$$\end{document}, particularly in safety-of-life applications.

The n-dimensional DIA-estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\underline{\text {x}}} = \sum ^k_{i=0} \hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_i\, p_i(\underline{\text {t}})$$\end{document} encompasses both statistical hypothesis testing and parameter estimation, components that are likewise fundamental to Advanced Receiver Autonomous Integrity Monitoring (ARAIM) and that are formulated under comparable modeling assumptions (Blanch et al. 2012, 2015). Different to Detection, Identification and Adaptation (DIA), ARAIM employs a testing strategy based on solution separation, primarily designed for detecting outliers in the observables (which is also the focus of the present study). Although the statistical testing procedures differ, the underlying objective is the same: assuming enough measurement redundancy formulate and use tests to select the most likely hypothesis \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}_i$$\end{document}, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{0,...,k\}$$\end{document}, and provide the corresponding estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_i \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}. Within the DIA-estimator framework, this corresponds to adopting a different partition structure of the misclosure space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document}. In this sense, the DIA-estimator framework represents a class of estimators of which ARAIM’s SS-based estimator is one member. In contrast to the usual upperbounding approach of the literature, this framework also enables an explicit evaluation of the probability of positioning failure, and we show how this can be done; in principle, the same approach could be used under the ARAIM solution-separation test. Solution separation is based on the estimator difference, or ’separation’, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_0 - \hat{\underline{\text {x}}}_{i \ne 0}$$\end{document}, which is linearly related to the misclosure vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\text {t}} \in \mathbb {R}^r$$\end{document}. In ARAIM, integrity performance is typically quantified using conservative upper bounds on integrity risk (Joerger and Pervan 2016), whereas our approach evaluates the probability of positioning failure itself \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}_{\mathcal {F}} = \textsf{P}\left( \overline{\underline{\text {x}}} \notin \mathcal {B} \right) $$\end{document} (with quantified numerical simulation uncertainty). Overall, the DIA-estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\underline{\text {x}}} \in \mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document} provides a general framework that is also applicable to ARAIM and may offer complementary capabilities for positioning safety evaluation.

Furthermore, the type of positioning-safety analysis, as presented in this work, is broadly applicable across a range of safety-critical applications (e.g., automotive, civil aviation, rail, maritime). For example, it can support the development of positioning algorithms and systems, and their verification with respect to safety and performance requirements, as well as help in studies aimed at defining or refining such requirements. Although the positioning safety analysis was illustrated using a representative TSO-certified GPS receiver in a UAV context, the example can be extended to incorporate multisensor fusion and additional GNSS constellations (e.g., Galileo), which will be the subject of future work.
